# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-20

## LLM Agents
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Can It Actually Work? Synthesizing Functional CAD Mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26861v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While LLM agents can generate visually plausible CAD assemblies, these designs often fail to fulfill their intended physical functions. We study functional CAD design, a setting where agents interpret underspecified requests to construct and iteratively revise mechanisms that satisfy functional requirements in physical simulation. Verifying these requirements is nontrivial: different candidate mechanisms may realize the same function through entirely different components, controls, and operating sequences. To bridge this gap, we propose FunCAD-Harness, a framework that translates abstract requirements into candidate-specific physical tests and uses simulation feedback to guide design revisions. To systematically evaluate this capability, we introduce FunCADBench, a benchmark spanning 46 mechanism families with interdependent and multi-step operations. Experiments across frontier LLM agents show that FunCAD-Harness exposes subtle mechanical failures invisible to visual inspection, substantially improving functional success rates over standard simulation-augmented baselines. Ultimately, our work marks a step toward CAD agents capable of translating human intent into working mechanisms.</p></details> | <details><summary>45 pa...</summary><p>45 pages, 16 figures, project page: https://thegenlab.ai/funcad/</p></details> |
| **[Same Simulation, Different Evidence: Where Retrieved Tool Output Costs LLM Agents Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26836v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents that compute the right answer can still answer wrongly. Agent harnesses increasingly keep long tool outputs out of the context window, saving them to files that the agent searches or reads on demand. We ask where this costs accuracy with a real scientific tool: PHREEQC, a widely used geochemistry simulator from the USGS, whose reports reach 846.8k characters and whose determinism lets every printed value be traced. Agents use it to answer 158 execution-verified multiple-choice items. When agents read the simulator's report through a section index rather than in context, GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2 lose 11.3 and 7.9 points of accuracy, while Claude Opus 4.6 and Claude Sonnet 4.6 show no detectable cost. Re-running all 11,007 logged simulator calls shows that both GPT models compute the keyed value about as often as before; what changes is the evidence they answer from. Under retrieval they often skip the report, answer from their own higher-precision output files, and misround to a neighboring option, a failure we call evidence substitution. With the simulation held fixed, retrieval still costs GPT-5.2 9.4 points. For GPT-5.4, more frequent correct simulations mask an evidence-use loss of about ten points, leaving a net change indistinguishable from zero. Naming the authoritative output raises accuracy by 6.3-7.9 points over plain retrieval for the three GPT models; for GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2, inlining outputs that fit works as well, while asking for correct rounding does not. Context-management choices in agent harnesses should be judged by where accuracy is lost, not only by final accuracy.</p></details> | 15 pages, 4 figures |
| **[Which Model for Which Role? Cost-Efficient Pareto Recommendation for LLM Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26676v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agentic workflows increasingly employ different models for specialized roles. Users may prefer different trade-offs between task quality and deployment cost, sometimes also considering latency. Finding good assignments incurs a separate search cost from evaluating multiple candidates. We introduce Cost-Coupled Gittins (CC-Gittins), a multi-objective extension of single-objective cost-aware Gittins search for Pareto recommendation. It exploits the coupling between evaluation and deployment costs: assignments that cost more to deploy also cost more to evaluate. The search alternates between seeking higher quality and lower deployment cost, reusing each evaluation to inform both searches. After each batch, CC-Gittins recommends assignments that are nondominated under uncertainty-adjusted estimates of quality and deployment cost. We evaluate CC-Gittins on eight quality--cost matrices from workflows with two to five roles and up to 1,681 candidate assignments. We also evaluate the method with latency as a third objective. Across the eight benchmarks, CC-Gittins recovers a substantial fraction of the true Pareto frontier at roughly 10% of the exhaustive-evaluation cost, and recovers most of the frontier with few false positives by 30%.</p></details> | <details><summary>Preli...</summary><p>Preliminary version accepted at NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on Resource-Aware Agentic AI (RAAAI)</p></details> |
| **[CoSec: Benchmarking Agent Security in Communities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34790v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents operate in persistent collaborative environments involving multiple users, communities, memories, files, and tools. Community boundaries may remain fixed or evolve with changes in membership, roles, composition, and relationships. Agents must complete legitimate tasks and prevent unauthorized disclosure of protected information. Existing evaluations do not fully examine these risks in agent systems. We introduce \textbf{CoSec}, an executable benchmark for evaluating privacy and authorization enforcement in LLM agent systems operating within and across communities. CoSec contains 208 canonical scenarios spanning fixed and evolving boundaries, protected information belonging to the agent owner or other participants, and attacks through dialogue, environmental content, persistent memory, and composed workflows. CoSec executes complete agent systems with persistent sessions, memory, files and tools. It verifies information flows against the active authorization state using execution traces and artifacts. Across harness and model configurations, agents frequently complete benign tasks but violate privacy and authorization boundaries. Privacy behavior varies across harnesses, attack surfaces, and community states, revealing how memory, files, tools, and workflows can carry protected information beyond its authorized scope. These findings show that task utility does not imply privacy or authorization compliance and that authorization in community settings remains an unresolved security challenge for persistent LLM agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[Agent-OM: Leveraging LLM Agents for Ontology Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.00326v30)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Ontology matching (OM) enables semantic interoperability between different ontologies and resolves their conceptual heterogeneity by aligning related entities. OM systems currently have two prevailing design paradigms: conventional knowledge-based expert systems and newer machine learning-based predictive systems. While large language models (LLMs) and LLM agents have revolutionised data engineering and have been applied creatively in many domains, their potential for OM remains underexplored. This study introduces a novel agent-powered LLM-based design paradigm for OM systems. With consideration of several specific challenges in leveraging LLM agents for OM, we propose a generic framework, namely Agent-OM (Agent for Ontology Matching), consisting of two Siamese agents for retrieval and matching, with a set of OM tools. Our framework is implemented in a proof-of-concept system. Evaluations of three Ontology Alignment Evaluation Initiative (OAEI) tracks over state-of-the-art OM systems show that our system can achieve results very close to the long-standing best performance on simple OM tasks and can significantly improve the performance on complex and few-shot OM tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>35 pa...</summary><p>35 pages - VLDB 2025 (Page 1-20), OM 2025 (Page 21-31), OM 2026 (Page 32-35)</p></details> |
| **[Fake News, Real Crashes: Information Injection Attacks on LLM Financial Trading Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26438v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents now trade real capital using news as evidence, yet they cannot reliably tell fabricated headlines from real news. Because they read the same feeds under near-identical policies, a single fabricated post can push them into the same trade at the same moment. We present a systematic attack benchmark for information injection against LLM trading agents. Unlike prompt injection, which targets the model's instructions, information injection targets the evidence the agent trusts. The benchmark spans five injection vectors and seven event categories, evaluated over ~10,000 decisions on 15 U.S. stocks. Five findings emerge. (i) Prompt injection achieves 14% attack success rate (ASR), but fabricated news reaches 62% and two 30-word templates reach 100% on all 15 stocks, inverting the usual LLM-security intuition. (ii) For fact-poisoning templates, surface form matters more than content, since removing credibility cues such as a named source or a "BREAKING" tag drops ASR from 100% to 0% while the fake claim remains identical. (iii) Success depends on the agent's state, with a fake indicator panel scoring 91% against an agent about to hold but 12% against one about to buy. (iv) Attacks generalize across model vendors and remain effective when blended, unlabeled, into real news feeds. (v) Most attacks evade standard defenses, since model scaling stops shallow vectors but not fact poisoning, peer debate fails, and only hierarchical aggregation reduces ASR.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 7 figures, 18 tables</p></details> |
| **[ActiveMem: Dynamic Latent Memory Trees for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33244v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on external memory to support long-horizon reasoning and decision making. Existing memory systems typically retrieve historical trajectories or summaries as independent context fragments, overlooking the procedural dependencies underlying multi-step execution. As memory scales, such flat retrieval introduces context fragmentation and cross-task interference, leading to structurally inconsistent reasoning trajectories. We propose ActiveMem, a hierarchical memory framework that recursively organizes agent experiences into dependency-aware latent execution trees. ActiveMem abstracts trajectories into reusable subtask nodes while explicitly preserving execution transitions, enabling coherent reasoning-path retrieval conditioned on the current execution state. To support continual adaptation, ActiveMem further learns dynamic memory expansion, retrieval, and pruning policies through reinforcement learning. Experiments across various agent benchmarks demonstrate that ActiveMem consistently improves task completion, reasoning stability, and memory efficiency over existing memory-based agents. Moreover, ActiveMem enables compact open-weight models to achieve competitive performance with substantially larger proprietary systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Sequential Behavioral Watermarking for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11036v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based agents act through sequences of executable decisions, but their trajectories provide little evidence of which agent or policy produced them, making provenance, ownership, and unauthorized reuse difficult to establish from observed behavior alone. This motivates watermarking signals embedded directly into agent behavior rather than only into generated text, since text watermarking cannot capture the action-level decisions that define agent execution. Recent agent watermarking methods address this gap by moving the watermark from generated text to behavioral choices. However, by treating each action step as an independent trial, they overlook trajectory structure and become fragile when trajectories are perturbed, truncated, or observed without reliable alignment. We propose SeqWM, a sequential behavioral watermarking framework that embeds signals into history-conditioned transition patterns and verifies trajectories position-agnostically against random-key baselines. Experiments across diverse agent benchmarks and LLM backbones show that SeqWM consistently achieves reliable detection while preserving agent utility, and remains robust under trajectory corruption where round-indexed behavioral watermarks collapse.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 3 figures, preprint</p></details> |
| **[AutoMind: Adaptive Knowledgeable Agent for Automated Data Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10974v4)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) agents have shown great potential in addressing real-world data science problems. LLM-driven data science agents promise to automate the entire machine learning pipeline, yet their real-world effectiveness remains limited. Existing frameworks depend on rigid, pre-defined workflows and inflexible coding strategies; consequently, they excel only on relatively simple, classical problems and fail to capture the empirical expertise that human practitioners bring to complex, innovative tasks. In this work, we introduce AutoMind, an adaptive, knowledgeable LLM-agent framework that overcomes these deficiencies through three key advances: (1) a curated expert knowledge base that grounds the agent in domain expert knowledge, (2) an agentic knowledgeable tree search algorithm that strategically explores possible solutions, and (3) a self-adaptive coding strategy that dynamically tailors code generation to task complexity. Evaluations on two automated data science benchmarks demonstrate that AutoMind delivers superior performance versus state-of-the-art baselines. Additional analyses confirm favorable effectiveness, efficiency, and qualitative solution quality, highlighting AutoMind as an efficient and robust step toward fully automated data science. Code is at https://github.com/zjunlp/AutoMind.</p></details> | DAI 2026 |
| **[Annotrace: A Workbench for Step-Level Annotation of Agent Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23284v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Every study of why LLM agents fail begins by labelling traces by hand, and every such study builds its own throwaway tool. Observability platforms record agent traces but offer no structured labelling; annotation tools label text but have no notion of a step, a delegation or a tool call, leaving it unclear what the smallest unit of execution to label should be. Annotrace reads six trace formats, including OpenTelemetry GenAI, LangSmith and Langfuse, and turns each run into an ordered, nested list of steps. The annotator writes their own labelling codebook; a frontier model proposes a starting set from a sample of their own traces, answers first, and is asked exactly the questions the human is asked, making annotation a joint human-AI process. Annotrace measures agreement between any two annotators, human or AI. On a multi-agent question-answering corpus it reproduces a previous manual effort's 235 annotation units exactly, and two models labelling the same traces agree 91% of the time at $κ= 0.82$.</p></details> | <details><summary>4 pag...</summary><p>4 pages, 4 figures, 1 table. Under review at the AAAI 2027 Demonstration Program</p></details> |
| **[Reliable Before Profitable: QuantHarness for Governing Financial Strategy Development with LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26101v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents can turn natural-language financial research requests into executable and backtested strategies, but a successful run may still implement the wrong rule, use information unavailable at decision time, or obscure how repairs and candidate selection changed the strategy. Because these failures arise throughout development, final code and performance metrics alone are insufficient to establish either fidelity to the request or the validity of the development process. We therefore address the reliability of financial strategy development at the trajectory level: the system must control how intermediate outputs become downstream state and preserve the evidence needed to justify the terminal outcome. We introduce QuantHarness, a two-layer agent harness in which a finance-specific Workflow structures specification, data grounding, construction, testing, and review, while Harness Core enforces stage-specific information, action, and commitment boundaries. We also introduce FinRelBench, a benchmark that evaluates strategy-development reliability through execution success, requirement satisfaction, and runtime evidence. QuantHarness achieves the highest Task Success at 90.72\%, compared with 74.23\%--84.54\% for the comparison systems. Controlled stress tests show that its boundaries contain targeted violations, while structured run records improve evidence retrieval and support decision replay under the evaluated protocol.</p></details> | <details><summary>47 pa...</summary><p>47 pages, 2 figures, including appendices</p></details> |
| **[FinHopper: A Progressive Multi-Hop Benchmark for Complex Financial Agents with Step-wise Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26036v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Financial agents are expected to answer factual investment queries through open search, but financial multi-hop retrieval differs from general multi-hop QA in a crucial way: each intermediate answer is an exact typed constraint, such as an entity, date, event, or numerical indicator, that determines the next query. This strict answer-conditioned dependency makes small intermediate errors cascade into plausible but wrong search trajectories. We introduce FinHopper, a benchmark for complex financial agents with controlled reasoning depth from 1 to 6 hops, covering stocks, funds, and macroeconomic facts, with gold intermediate answers for process-level evaluation. To construct FinHopper at scale, we design a state-transition-based synthesis framework that composes verified atomic financial QA patterns into dependency-preserving chains, followed by targeted in-synthesis verification, model-ensemble checking, and public-search validation by financial experts. Experiments with 20 LLM-agent configurations show that public search tools are necessary but insufficient: performance drops sharply as hop depth increases, the best model remains far below human experts, and process-aware scoring reveals partial reasoning progress hidden by final-answer-only evaluation. The benchmark is available at https://github.com/justajustin/FinHopper/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to EMNLP 2026. 28 pages, 7 figures, 8 tables. Benchmark: https://github.com/justajustin/FinHopper/</p></details> |
| **[Productive State-Space Exploration for Multi-turn RL in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25957v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement Learning (RL) for Large Language Model (LLM) agents in multi-turn, long-horizon environments remains challenging due to inefficient action selection and repetitive behavioral patterns. In particular, agents often become trapped in high-confidence yet unproductive behaviors, repeatedly executing invalid actions or entering action loops that hinder task completion. We observe that these frequently violate environment preconditions, revealing a mismatch between the agent's representation of the internal state and the dynamics of the underlying environment. In addition, repetitive high-confidence actions lead to cyclical behaviors that provide little to no progress towards the goal. We argue that addressing these challenges requires more productive task-aligned exploration to improve the agent's understanding of environment dynamics. Motivated by intrinsic curiosity-driven exploration, we propose a conditional state-space exploration framework that balances task completion with intrinsic information-seeking behavior. Specifically, we introduce an intrinsic reward that penalizes actions whose observation duplicates prior state memory. This provides the benefit of reducing redundant behaviors and encouraging state-space coverage without relying on a general information-theoretic measure. Strong experimental results demonstrate that our approach substantially improves policy learning in multi-turn environments, mitigating invalid actions and repetitive loops.</p></details> |  |
| **[CXBench: A Benchmark for Process-Compliant LLM Agents in Regulated Customer-Experience Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25954v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using language agents increasingly perform regulated customer-service workflows, where a correct final state is insufficient: agents must also provide disclosures, obtain consent, and follow required procedures. Existing tool-agent benchmarks largely score final business states, accepting trajectories that reach correct outcomes through non-compliant processes. We introduce cxbench, a benchmark that evaluates outcome and process compliance separately across six regulated domains. Each task combines a customer request, a tool-based environment, and explicit process requirements. Rule cards link cited regulatory obligations to observable trace evidence. A hybrid grader combines deterministic checks for ordering and authority with an LLM judge for semantic requirements. We define a false-pass as an outcome-pass run that fails process checks. Across seven models and 120 tasks, 55.5% of the 840 retained executions are false-passes, representing 80.1% of outcome-passing runs; only 13.8% pass both checks. Process-inclusive evaluation changes the observed model ordering and exposes differences hidden by outcome-only scoring. cxbench makes these failures inspectable through trace-linked criteria.</p></details> |  |
| **[AdaExploreBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Proactive Exploration and Test-time Adaptation in Evolving Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25950v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in unfamiliar and evolving environments, requiring them to proactively explore, understand, and adapt to environmental dynamics. However, existing benchmarks rarely test whether agents can proactively discover how an environment works and adapt as it evolves. To address this gap, we introduce AdaExploreBench, a benchmark for proactive exploration and test-time adaptation in evolving environments. By instantiating environmental dynamics as hidden rules, AdaExploreBench requires agents to actively acquire evidence and infer the rules. Under implicit rule drift, the latent rule changes without announcement, requiring agents to detect from degraded performance that prior knowledge is stale and re-explore. Experiments on eight leading LLMs show that proactive and adaptive exploration remain challenging: GPT-6 Sol achieves only 61.9% calibrated accuracy, while providing sufficient evidence or removing rule drift improves average performance significantly by close to 10%. These results reveal a critical bottleneck in current LLM agents: they can often reason effectively from informative evidence once it is available, but struggle to actively acquire the right evidence and recognize when previously acquired evidence is no longer valid.</p></details> |  |
| **[CyberArena: Benchmarking AI Agents in Live Symmetric Attack-and-Defense Combat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19005v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents are demonstrating increasingly strong offensive and defensive cybersecurity capabilities in the real world. Current benchmarks mostly evaluate agents in isolation against static targets. Real cyber operations, however, are dynamic and coupled: security postures constantly change as attackers and defenders adapt to each other in real time. Static benchmarks quickly saturate and miss an agent's ability to attack and defend simultaneously under live pressure. To address this gap, we present CyberArena, a dynamic, symmetric adversarial benchmark built around multi-round attack-and-defense combat. In isolated sandboxes, two agents compete under identical rules, acting concurrently as both attackers and defenders over continuously changing services. This converts static targets into evolving, semi-open challenges, directly testing an agent's speed and precision in discovering, exploiting, and patching vulnerabilities. To sustain the benchmark's longevity, an automated agentic pipeline continuously generates, reviews, and validates new environments. Furthermore, because pairwise contests are notoriously hard to quantify, we introduce a dynamical model that maps match dynamics into unified, multidimensional scores across offensive, defensive, and domain-specific capabilities. Evaluating 12 frontier LLM agents across 40 challenges in 466 matches, CyberArena separates model capabilities where single-agent baselines hit a ceiling. The trajectories read like a fight rather than an exam: defenders patch one hole and miss the next and certify repairs on stale evidence, while capable agents think a move ahead, planting backdoors for later rounds and, in one case, writing their own packet sniffer to watch the opponent. Beyond a benchmark, CyberArena serves as an adaptable, scalable experimental platform for future research on cybersecurity agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[Maestro: Agentic Formal Proof Development by Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25920v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Lean is increasingly used to verify mathematical proofs and software produced by LLM agents, but writing formal proofs remains hard. Existing prover systems take a divide-and-conquer approach that recursively decomposes hard proof obligations into easier lemmas and splits again any lemma that resists proof. However, a lemma can resist proof for different reasons, including insufficient guidance, a false statement, or a flaw in the decomposition that produced it. Existing systems cannot tell these apart as a failed attempt reports little beyond its failure, and responses are confined to the failing node without considering the rest of the proof or revising the plan above it. The system may thus keep decomposing obligations along a flawed proof direction until the budget runs out, causing the whole proof attempt to fail. We introduce Maestro, a Lean prover harness that handles failures through global coordination. Provers report why they failed, not just that they failed, and a coordinator that sees the whole proof chooses an appropriate response, such as guiding a stuck prover, correcting a false lemma, or retracting a flawed subtree and replanning it. The same harness extends to verified code synthesis, where a checked counterexample sends the code back for repair. We evaluate Maestro with four frontier LLMs on seven mathematics and three software verification benchmarks. It proves more targets than each model's native coding agent (Claude Code or Codex) on every benchmark the agent has not already saturated, with the largest gains on research-level mathematics. With Claude Opus 5, it discovers its own proofs of 34 of the 48 theorems in the June 2026 release of arXivLean, against 19 for the native agent. It also outperforms all published prover systems on every benchmark they share, solving 669/672 PutnamBench, 59/60 Lean-IMO-Bench, and 6/6 IMO 2026.</p></details> |  |
| **[AURA-Eval: Evaluation Framework for Acting Under Risk Awareness in LLM Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06783v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are increasingly taking actions with real consequences, yet safety evaluations often reduce their behavior to a final safe/unsafe outcome, obscuring whether the agent recognized the risk and how it responded. We introduce AURA-Eval, a framework for evaluating whether agents recognize contextual risk and adapt their behavior accordingly. AURA-Eval truncates tool-use trajectories at safety-critical decision points and asks the evaluated model to generate the next thought and action. It creates controlled scenario variations along six risk mechanisms: harm intensity, scale, target susceptibility, system dependency, oversight, and reversibility. It also creates paired scenarios that preserve the underlying goal but differ in whether safe fulfillment is available, allowing unsafe compliance and over-refusal to be evaluated together. An LLM-judge panel, validated against human annotations, evaluates each continuation along three axes: risk detection from the reasoning trace, and action type and safety from the action. From 157 R-Judge seed trajectories, we construct 1,249 evaluation items and evaluate 20 frontier, open-weight, and safety-tuned models. Across all 20 models, unsafe-action rates are higher when no safe path for request fulfillment is available than when one is; for frontier models, the rates are 27-40% versus 5-7%. When a safe path exists, frontier models rarely refuse; when none exists, they most often propose an alternative. Open-weight models, by contrast, more often execute the request than propose alternatives and act unsafely on 58-83% of items. Controlled risk-mechanism analyses further show particularly high unsafe rates under changes to oversight and scale; removing pre-execution oversight flips 52.3% of cases that were safe on the corresponding original scenario to unsafe.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Finding to Fixing: How Useful is Fault Localization for Agentic Program Repair?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25826v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fault localization (FL) aims to identify code locations responsible for software failures and is commonly evaluated by how accurately it ranks known fault locations. Large language model (LLM) agents are changing program repair and are increasingly used to search and reason over repositories. Although recent LLM-based FL methods have improved localization accuracy, their impact on agentic repair remains unclear. To address this gap, we conduct a systematic empirical study of FL in agentic repair. We evaluate six recent FL methods on SWE-bench Verified and Defects4J with three LLMs under a common repair framework and analyze 16,248 repair trajectories. We also examine gold locations and bug-cause descriptions as forms of FL guidance across different repair contexts. The studied FL methods do not consistently improve repair success, and higher localization accuracy does not consistently lead to larger repair gains. Yet FL guides agents' early search and helps them reach relevant code sooner. Accurate locations can reduce repair token use by up to 39.2\%, but current FL methods often consume more tokens than they save during repair. Experiments with gold guidance suggest that FL has greater potential when bug inputs contain fewer location clues. Providing bug causes can further improve repair success beyond locations alone. We also find that ground truth from a single reference patch does not fully capture the locations used by successful repairs. These findings help clarify the value of FL in agentic repair and inform future work on its design and evaluation.</p></details> |  |
| **[CoMem: Learning to Anticipate Failures from Retrieved Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25790v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reflection lets LLM agents improve from their own attempts, but the memory it builds is local to a single task, so an agent reflecting on its own tends to revisit the same explanation for failure. Cross-task reflective memory addresses this in two forms: retrieved memory keeps past experience explicit and looks up related cases, which is specific but bounded by the bank, and parametric memory internalizes experience into parameters, which generalizes but is grounded in nothing beyond the task description. When both are used, existing agents combine them additively, feeding their outputs to the solver side by side, so retrieved experience never changes what the learned memory produces. We introduce CoMem (Compounding Memory), which makes retrieved experience an input to the learned memory itself, at training and at inference. The agent builds a bank of its own successful and failed reflections, learns from retrieved cases to write failure hypotheses, candidate explanations of how an attempt is likely to go wrong, and at test time reasons from newly retrieved cases to guide each attempt. Varying the retrieved cases gives the memory different perspectives, yielding reflective diversity that is not merely sampled but also grounded in retrieved experience. Across two backbones and five benchmarks in math, code, and multi-hop QA, CoMem achieves strong overall performance. Additional evaluations show that it generalizes to out-of-distribution tasks and enables smaller models to provide effective guidance to stronger solvers.</p></details> | 29 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Standing on the Shoulders of Giants: Enhancing LLM Agents for Automated Fact-Checking with Human Expert Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25777v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated fact-checking (AFC) systems powered by large language model (LLM) agents still face challenges in evidence retrieval and claim verification. Existing approaches improve their capabilities through computationally costly post-training or human-in-the-loop feedback that is difficult to scale. To transfer professional fact-checking expertise to LLM agents efficiently, we distill publicly available guidelines published by Agence France-Presse (AFP) Fact Check into a reusable, modular Professional Fact-Checking Skill (PFC-Skill). We then present SkillCheck, the first AFC system that gives LLM agents dynamic access to PFC-Skill to guide evidence retrieval and claim verification at inference time. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SkillCheck improves upon skill-free DEFAME baselines across multiple LLM backbones, with gains of 2.2--4.8 pp. in Accuracy and 1.5--6.3 pp. in Macro-F1 on the AVeriTeC benchmark. Notably, SkillCheck with GPT-4o-Mini exceeds DEFAME with GPT-4o by 3.4 pp. in Accuracy while cutting inference cost by roughly 61%. The performance gains also extend to open-web retrieval. Ablations show that incorporating the skill during evidence retrieval provides most of the gains, while incorporation into both stages yields the best overall performance. Case studies show that PFC-Skill enables more targeted search and closer scrutiny of evidence. To conclude, our work shows that LLM agents can "stand on the shoulders of giants" by drawing on the best practices of professional fact-checkers, substantially enhancing AFC performance without extra training or continuous human supervision.</p></details> |  |
| **[Computation, Not Memory: Chain-of-Thought and State Tracking in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25760v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language-model agents often reason before they act, and the value of monitoring that reasoning depends on how it relates to their actions. We ask how an agent's actions depend on its reasoning when the correct action depends on a changing state. In a small file-system task, background events announce which file's lock has been toggled but not its new value. The agent therefore has to reconstruct the current locks from its history before it can move files safely. With Qwen3-8B and Qwen3.5-9B, we separate two roles of reasoning: generating it before each action, and retaining earlier reasoning in the prompt. Most of the reduction in lock-violating commands comes from reasoning written at the moment of action. Retaining earlier reasoning adds little to rule-following, although it helps one model complete the task. The benefit depends on what the reasoning says. Filler text, reasoning written for another history and an accurate table of the state do not reproduce it. When we give the agent reasoning written for a history that differs in one lock value, the agent acts on that reasoning even against its own history. Without reasoning, linear probes recover the state from the model's activations about as well as a text classifier recovers it from the transcript. However, decoding accuracy does not predict which interventions change the agent's actions. In this setting, the benefit of reasoning for rule-following comes mainly from generating it at each step. | **[Explore or Confirm? LLM Agents Misallocate Measurements Under Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25111v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As model reasoning gets cheaper, the physical experiment becomes the slow and costly step in discovery. Each experiment returns a noisy reading: a high value can mean a good candidate or a lucky draw. With a fixed budget, an AI agent must keep choosing between exploring a new candidate and confirming a promising one by re-testing it. We measure that choice in Wellocate, a simulated assay campaign with known true values. It is built on a generated landscape and on two real protein datasets, GB1 and PhoQ, scrambled so no model can answer from memory. We test five language models as minimal agents, with no tools and no memory beyond the history in each prompt. They misallocate in both directions. Three over-confirm, spending most of the budget re-testing. On the generated landscape one under-confirms, trusting single readings, and there every model ends with a worse final pick than a basic scripted strategy. Each failure has its own cause. The over-confirmers run short of new candidates worth trying: a list of untried candidates cuts their re-testing and, on real protein data, makes two of them pick much better. A running summary of their readings or a description of the landscape changes it by at most five percentage points. For the under-confirmer, re-testing its top candidates in a final round, replayed on its own campaigns, recovers only about a quarter of what it loses by picking the wrong candidate. This suggests confirmation has to run through the whole campaign. We release Wellocate as a benchmark and reinforcement-learning environment with frozen splits and scripted baselines. With it, anyone can measure how an agent splits its budget before trusting it with real experiments, and train agents to split it better. Code, data and the environment are available at https://github.com/Xstendyl/wellocate.</p></details> | <details><summary>38 pa...</summary><p>38 pages, 12 figures. Code, data and environment: https://github.com/Xstendyl/wellocate</p></details> |
| **[Toward Agentic Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Enterprise Retail Workflows over Heterogeneous Data Sources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25096v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise retail workflows require agents to chain evidence across heterogeneous sources, from structured product and order records to unstructured inquiries and reviews, yet existing agentic RAG benchmarks rarely capture this challenge. To enable controlled evaluation of such workflows, we introduce RetailBridge, a compositional retail benchmark built from persona-driven simulations. Its questions are constructed by linking entities and intermediate answers across heterogeneous sources, requiring agents to identify the relevant sources, carry evidence between them, and produce a grounded answer. To systematically evaluate LLMs on these retail workflows, we develop RetailRAG, a retail-aware framework equipped with auto-schema briefing, source-routed plan chaining, and diagnostic plan revision. RetailRAG consistently outperforms existing agentic RAG baselines across all evaluation axes, serving as a strong reference system for assessing LLM agents on retail workflows. On RetailBridge, evaluating ten LLM backbones with RetailRAG reveals multi-source chaining as the dominant bottleneck across providers and model scales, suggesting that reliable retail agents require explicit cross-source orchestration rather than scaling alone. Code and data are publicly available at \url{https://github.com/DISL-Lab/RetailBridge}.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prepr...</summary><p>Preprint. Under review</p></details> |
| **[PAAC: Privacy-Aware Agentic Device-Cloud Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08646v2)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents face a structural tension: cloud agents provide strong reasoning but expose user data, while on-device agents preserve privacy at the cost of overall capability. Existing device-cloud designs treat this boundary as a compute split rather than a trust boundary suited to agentic workloads, and existing sanitizers force a choice between policy flexibility and the structural fidelity tool calls require. In this work, we develop PAAC, a privacy-aware agentic framework that aligns planner--executor decomposition with the device-cloud boundary so that role specialization itself becomes the privacy mechanism. The cloud agent reasons over typed placeholder tokens that preserve each sensitive value's reasoning role while discarding its content, while the on-device agent identifies sensitive spans and distills each step's execution outcome into compact key findings. Sanitization confines the on-device LLM to proposing which spans to mask, while a deterministic registry performs all substitution and reversal, keeping actions directly executable on device. On three agentic benchmarks under strict privacy settings, PAAC dominates the Pareto frontier of privacy and accuracy, improving average accuracy by 15-36\% and reducing average leakage by 2-6$\times$ over state-of-the-art device-cloud baselines, with the largest margins on privacy targets outside fixed entity taxonomies. We find consistent improvements on 17 additional benchmarks spanning 10 domains, including math, science, and finance.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[LLM Agents as Suppliers of Physical System Models: Calibration and Acceptance in Modelica](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25019v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To procure a model of a device, a client hands a supplier a description, sometimes with recorded data or a target response, and accepts the model against this handover. We let LLM coding agents act as the supplier on handovers with data, building Modelica models from library components for four simulated systems and a real switching magnet, and ask what acceptance certifies. Several agents reach the noise floor, but a handover admits a set of models: the description leaves parts of the structure open, and the data narrow it only to the agreed accuracy. We therefore accept by membership in this set, not by matching one reference, and add layers above behaviour: a physical invariant exposes an energy-creating model at the noise floor. Interpretability becomes a property of the delivered model, tested on a variant, read where none exists and shaped through the handover. Most failures are fits delivered with a systematic residual on their own fitting data, where the handover left open how close is close enough. In a follow-up, stating a threshold makes the agents compare against it, and more deliveries pass. The argument applies wherever a handover underspecifies a model meant to be read, not only run.</p></details> |  |
| **[MemPoison: Uncovering Persistent Memory Threats and Structural Blind Spots in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14651v2)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Persistent external memory enhances agent continuity but introduces persistent security vulnerabilities: adversarial content can be injected via standard interaction channels, retained across turns, and later distort downstream behavior. To address this challenge, we propose MemPoison, a comprehensive benchmark and analysis framework featuring 1227 hand-validated cases across four attack types, three injection channels, and three representative memory substrates, evaluated on seven open-weight and three closed-weight model families. We introduce a three-tier taxonomy: (L1) direct single-record corruption, (L2) compositional multi-record corruption and (L3) context-triggered dormant corruption. Our evaluations reveal a distinct defense frontier: while baseline write-time defenses, such as consistency checks, substantially suppress direct L1 attacks, they fail to reliably suppress L2 and L3 attacks. Through mechanistic influence decomposition (MID), we demonstrate structural blind spots in write-time defenses, which admit seemingly benign records that later become harmful through joint retrieval composition or trigger-conditioned activation. Our findings advocate for shifting from static filtering to adaptive, context-sensitive memory defense strategies.</p></details> |  |
| **[CorrectRTL: Boost RTL Generation Quality Through Automated Self-Correction with Instruction Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24977v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong promise in specification-to-RTL generation. Existing benchmarks are saturated by advanced LLMs, leaving little room to evaluate the agentic self-correction framework. The monolithic self-correction paradigm rewrites the entire design at once, where LLMs are susceptible to error confusion and corruption of originally correct code. To address the benchmark challenge, we contribute ScaleBench, a more challenging benchmark of 200 complex RTL designs. To address the challenge in methodology, we propose CorrectRTL, an agentic framework that realizes a new decompose-then-correct paradigm, in which the LLM agent explicitly decouples scaffold construction from detailed implementation. CorrectRTL contributes three core techniques: (1)~\emph{Structure-oriented decomposition}, (2)~\emph{Snippet-based RAG}, and (3)~\emph{SVA-based self-correction}. On ScaleBench, CorrectRTL improves Pass@1 from 20.5% to 49.0% (+28.5%). | **[Can LLM Agents Respond to Disasters? Benchmarking Heterogeneous Geospatial Reasoning in Emergency Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11633v2)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Operational disaster response goes beyond damage assessment, requiring responders to integrate multi-sensor signals, reason over road networks, populations and key facilities, plan evacuations, and produce actionable reports. However, prior work largely isolates remote-sensing perception or evaluates generic tool use, leaving the end-to-end workflows of emergency operations underexplored. In this paper, we introduce Disaster Operational Response Agent benchmark (DORA), the first agentic benchmark for end-to-end disaster response: 515 expert-authored tasks across 45 real-world disaster events spanning 10 types, paired with expert-verified, replayable gold trajectories totaling 3,500 tool-call steps. Tasks span five dimensions that cover the operational disaster-response pipeline: disaster perception, spatial relational analysis, rescue and evacuation planning, temporal evolution reasoning, and multi-modal report synthesis. Agents compose calls from a 108-tool MCP library over heterogeneous geospatial data: optical, SAR, and multi-spectral imagery across single-, bi-, and multi-temporal sequences (0.015-10m GSD), complemented by elevation and social vector layers. We comprehensively evaluate 13 frontier LLMs on our benchmark, revealing three persistent challenges: 1) disaster-domain grounding exposes unique failure modes (damage-semantic grounding, sensor-modality mismatch, and disaster-pipeline composition); 2) agents are doubly bottlenecked by tool selection and argument grounding, where gold tool-order hints improve accuracy by only 1.08-4.40%, and alternative scaffolds yield at most a 3.24% gain; 3) compositional fragility scales with trajectory length, the agent-to-gold gap widening from 7% to 56% on long pipelines. | **[MemPrice: Online Memory Allocation via Adaptive Pricing for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24955v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon LLM agents face a fundamental allocation problem as interaction histories grow: which information to retain, how much context to devote to it, and at what granularity. MemPrice turns accumulated memory pressure into an adaptive price on context, encouraging detail only when its estimated retention value justifies the additional footprint. This pressure persists across memory refreshes, allowing the contents of memory to evolve while preserving long-run control over context usage. We show that irreversible compression can force linear worst-case regret against hindsight following its own memory trajectory, and introduce a same-state benchmark to evaluate allocation on MemPrice's realized memory states. MemPrice enforces the hard context budget at every round and regulates long-run occupancy through adaptive pricing, without prior knowledge of the interaction horizon. Under priced-frontier regularity, its average allocation-regret bound against this benchmark vanishes when accumulated estimation error is sublinear. We evaluate the framework under matched memory budgets on LongMemEval, LoCoMo, AppWorld, and WebShop across three foundation models, spanning both query-blind memory construction and goal-directed agent context management.</p></details> |  |
| **[HybridResearcher: Bridging the Structured-Unstructured Boundary for Deep Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24899v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While LLM agents are increasingly capable of gathering and synthesizing evidence from a single homogeneous source, such as structured tables or unstructured data, real-world analytical questions frequently span heterogeneous sources. For example, answering one enterprise question may require both a result computed over an internal database and evidence found on the web. To address this, we introduce HybridResearcher, a 4B agent trained to bridge evidence across the structured and unstructured boundary. To build it, we synthesize cross-source bridge trajectories by starting with validated single-source tasks-such as existing text-to-SQL or web search queries-and fabricating the missing source around a bridge entity drawn from the seed's own output. Evaluated on the existing HybridDeepResearch benchmark, HybridResearcher improves over our base model, Qwen3.5-4B, by 30.0 points pass@8 and surpasses the far larger Qwen3.5-397B-A17B by 8.3 points, while also improving over its base model on established, held-out single-source benchmarks (BrowseComp and BrowseComp-Plus), demonstrating that this complex cross-source reasoning can be taught directly to small models without triggering catastrophic forgetting.</p></details> |  |
| **[The One-Pixel of Reasoning: One Truthful Relation Is Enough to Mislead LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24873v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Can one sentence manipulate an agent's belief by targeting a single implicit warrant linking evidence to a conclusion? In this paper, we introduce the one-relation attack (ORA), a novel cognitive attack on this inferential relation. ORA localizes a critical implicit warrant and performs a one-relation intervention using a single source-grounded sentence that qualifies this warrant and steers the agent toward an incorrect belief. Across eighteen models on biomedical inference and vulnerability detection, best-of-$10 attacks overturn initially correct answers at mean rates of 54.9% for proprietary and 80.0% for open-weight models. | **[Bellman Language Models: Solving the Recurring Decisions of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24871v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A general observation in agentic workflows, especially bounded ones, is that the decision space of the LLM, when we separate a decision from generation, starts approaching saturation: the agent meets the same states again and again, and after enough conversations it stops meeting new ones. LLMs generally don't treat these decisions as an optimization problem and instead re-derive them from their context/prompt on every turn. We show that treating decision making within reasonable bounds as an optimization problem, solving it once, and reusing the solved decisions can often make an agent faster, cheaper and more reliable. Our approach, which we call Discretize, Then Decide, starts from data such as the workflow's own logs or, when none exists, runs of the LLM agent itself. It reads each conversation into discrete states and estimates the probability of each outcome and the cost of each move, until no new states appear and the probabilities stop changing. It then solves the resulting Markov decision process. The solution runs as a controller around the model, sending moves whose content is fixed in the state, such as a tool call or a templated reply, and any state it hasn't seen is sent back to the LLM. We call an LLM run this way a Bellman language model (BLM), after Richard Bellman's equation for the best move in each state. On $τ^2$-bench telecom, the controller with no model solves 79% of tasks. As a BLM, Qwen3.8-27B, an open model, goes from 29% to 96% with 93% fewer model calls. For Claude Opus 5.5, which solves 96% of tasks on its own, running it as a BLM cuts model calls and cost per task by about 98% and lets it solve 2 more tasks. On InventoryBench, the solved policy with no model beats every published method. Where the hard part is writing content rather than choosing moves, such as resolving SymPy issues, few decisions can be solved in advance, and a BLM helps little.</p></details> | <details><summary>52 pa...</summary><p>52 pages, 13 figures, 11 tables. Preregistrations: https://github.com/adarsh-vatsa/dtd-preregistration</p></details> |
| **[Pair-Aware Optimization: Coordinating Exploration and Refinement for Jailbreaking LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24823v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) agents are vulnerable to adversarial inputs that induce attacker-specified malicious behaviors, commonly referred to as jailbreaking. Existing optimization-based jailbreak attacks primarily employ first-order (FO) methods that leverage gradient information or zeroth-order (ZO) methods that rely on function-value feedback. However, their complementarity and optimization ordering remain underexplored. We systematically investigate four strategies: pure ZO, pure FO, ZO->FO, and FO->ZO, and develop a ZO-FO framework that explores adversarial prefixes through ZO optimization and refines their corresponding suffixes through FO optimization. To coordinate the two processes, we introduce Pair-aware optimization, which retains multiple prefix candidates, conditionally refines their suffixes and selects among the resulting prefix-suffix pairs. We further investigate agent jailbreaking from a more general stochastic-generation perspective, where the same adversarial input can induce diverse reasoning trajectories and actions that a single greedy output cannot capture. Accordingly, we adopt consistent stochastic decoding during both optimization and evaluation. Experiments across malicious-instruction and malicious-environment benchmarks demonstrate that hybrid optimization improves attack effectiveness over single-optimizer methods, while optimization order substantially influences performance.</p></details> |  |

In a fully logged reflection re-run, responses that both express doubt and delete are rare after a reflection instruction (25 of 509 executions), and a casual request that gives a reason but claims no authority is executed by all ten endpoints. Paper only. v2 shortens the paper, retitles it, corrects the diverse-prompt scoring (12.9% for delete calls on the protected path or a parent; 1.21% under the v1 rule), narrows "recognition" to decodable surface cues plus elicited doubt, and adds reflection controls and a sandbox test of external enforcement. | **[Toward Safe LLM Agents: A Survey of Specification, Verification, and Enforcement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14590v2)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) agents take irreversible real-world actions, yet no system gives formally grounded, task-level safety guarantees for their plans. We examined this gap in a PRISMA 2020 systematic review of six databases (20-29 May 2026) covering LLM agent specification, verification, and enforcement (2022-2026). We narratively synthesized 38 studies, assessed risk of bias per study, and graded evidence certainty with GRADE. First, specification is the bottleneck: natural-language-to-formal translation reaches only 24.8-34.2% semantic correctness on one Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL) benchmark. Second, runtime monitoring is the most mature enforcement strategy, reducing unsafe actions by 65.37% in one household-agent study, without complete guarantees. Third, in one $τ$-Bench study of transactional tool-use agents, blocking 94% of unsafe actions left safe task completion below 5%, as agents found unsafe alternatives (the verifier tax). Fourth, no approach simultaneously achieves soundness, completeness, scalability, semantic grounding, and utility preservation. We contribute a three-level taxonomy and a ten-problem research agenda.</p></details> | <details><summary>37 pa...</summary><p>37 pages, 2 figures, 11 tables. Revised version under review at ACM Computing Surveys. Data deposit: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23376335. PRISMA 2020 checklist included as an ancillary file</p></details> |
| **[ReTRACE: Feedback-Guided LLM Agent in a Chemistry Harness for Retrosynthesis Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24744v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computer-aided retrosynthesis has advanced through single-step prediction, tree search and route scoring, but complex targets still require judgement over the whole route, where stereochemistry, functional-group compatibility, cost and greenness interact. Each decoy combines a CVE-derived vulnerability chain that attracts verification with a false bridge that keeps its dangerous sink unreachable. | **[Virtual Speech Therapist: A Clinician-in-the-Loop AI Agent for Speech Therapy Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01101v3)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a proof of concept of the Virtual Speech Therapist (VST), an intelligent multi-agent based system designed to support stuttering assessment and individualized therapy planning through artificial intelligence (AI) driven workflows. These mechanisms enable the skill library to continuously accumulate useful experience while preserving and pruning skills across tasks, improving agent performance on complex tasks across diverse domains. | **[ATTUNER: Recomputation-Free Agentic Context KV Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36722v2)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents repeatedly load reusable content, such as skills, documents, and memory entries, into the current context. Extensive experiments on WildClawBench demonstrate that Darwin-Claw yields substantial performance improvements, establishing a controllable and stable pathway for the continual, user-centric adaptation of autonomous agents in real-world ecosystems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Mizzle: A Complete Concurrent Incorrectness Logic with Applications to Agentic Bug Certification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11611v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models are increasingly used to find bugs in real-world programs, but they also produce a flood of false alarms that waste developers' time. Unlike a test, such a proof is unaffected by how rarely a concurrency bug manifests, and can certify a bug against a library's specification rather than its implementation. We follow the approach of incorrectness logics, whose under-approximate reasoning establishes that a claimed behavior is genuinely reachable, and hence a true positive. In our case, however, the logic must model a realistic programming language, be mechanized so that proofs can be checked, and be complete, so that no real bug is ruled out for want of a derivation. We present Mizzle, an incorrectness separation logic for concurrent programs written in a substantial subset of OCaml, parametric in the notion of incorrectness. We mechanize Mizzle in the Rocq proof assistant on top of the Iris framework, and we prove that it is both sound, so that it never justifies a false alarm, and complete, so that every incorrect execution admits a derivation. We instantiate Mizzle with three notions of incorrectness: stuckness (triggering undefined behavior), the presence of a memory race (in a sequentially consistent setting), and the non-linearizability of a data structure. We show that an LLM agent can use Mizzle to certify seeded memory races and linearizability bugs for under a dollar per bug on average. | **[Wait for It: A Component-Level Analysis of Latency Feedback Mechanisms for LLM Agents in VR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23336v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Delays in AI responses pose a significant challenge to immersive Virtual Reality experiences. We propose RIT-RAG (Retrieval-Induced Tree RAG), which combines content retrieval with structural navigation. Offline, RIT-RAG builds a tree for each document from its table of contents or sitemap. Across financial, scientific, and customer-support benchmarks, RIT-RAG achieves the highest answer accuracy among vanilla, graph-based, and agentic baselines. This pattern also persists in an autonomous retrieval setup across all four models tested in this setting. Requiring answers in the page language increases pooled harmfulness on matched tool calls from 50.3% to 57.4% across four closed-weight models, mainly in Chinese and Hindi. | **[ParetoChat: An LLM-Based Interface for Interactive Multi-Objective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23062v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective optimization models are often deployed in settings where decision makers (DMs) must balance some conflicting criteria. This paper proposes \emph{ParetoChat}, an LLM (large language model)-based expert-agent interface for interactive multi-objective optimization. The agent translates natural-language user input into structured scalarization parameters, invokes an optimization system, presents candidate solutions, and iteratively elicits feedback. We describe a multi-agent architecture that separates input processing, multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM) expertise, solution presentation, and feedback processing. We then present a simulation study using an LLM-based DM agent and benchmark ParetoChat against interactive methods implemented in DESDEO, including reference-point and NIMBUS methods. ## Medical Large Language Models
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[PFedQFT: Personalized Federated Quantum-Inspired Fine-Tuning for Medical Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25712v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical data remain fragmented across institutional silos because of privacy, governance, and ownership constraints. Federated learning enables medical institutions to adapt large language models (LLMs) collaboratively without transferring raw records. However, independently parameterized low-rank adapters incur repeated parameter and communication costs across Transformer projections, while uniform aggregation may limit adaptation to heterogeneous institutional data. We propose PFedQFT, a quantum-inspired framework for personalized federated fine-tuning with a compact shared adapter. Specifically, PFedQFT constructs reusable orthonormal adaptation bases from compact skew-symmetric generators using a truncated matrix-exponential approximation followed by QR orthonormalization. Compatible projections reuse these bases, while a globally shared full core preserves the flexibility of each projection. Each client further maintains a lightweight private residual operator, whose directions are learned globally and whose coefficients remain local. Experiments on seven public medical tasks and multiple LLM backbones show substantial reductions in trainable parameters and per-round adapter upload volume, with competitive predictive performance on most tasks.</p></details> | 10 pages, 5 figures |
| **[TriageRA-CCF: Source-Side Clinical Confidence and Coverage Signals for Adaptive Rank Budgeting in Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29375v2)** | 2026-09-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical large language models are commonly adapted with a fixed low-rank budget, even though medical questions differ substantially in confidence, clinical coverage, and cross-domain difficulty. We study adaptive rank budgeting for parameter-efficient medical question answering: for each question, the adapter decides whether to activate a small, medium, or large subset of LoRA rank channels. The central challenge is that a naive adaptive budget router can collapse to unstable choices or spend capacity without improving shifted benchmarks. We propose TriageRA-CCF, a source-side teacher for adaptive rank-budgeted LoRA. It combines three signals computed only from source training data: base-model answer confidence, metadata-cell clinical coverage, and a counterfactual close-miss proxy. These signals supervise a straight-through budget router over active ranks {2,4,8}, together with budget-cost, entropy, and rank-balance regularization. Under a matched CMB-source training protocol, TriageRA-CCF achieves the best average accuracy among LoRA, DoRA, and MoELoRA baselines on both Qwen3-8B and Llama3.1-8B. The gains are modest and non-uniform across benchmarks: +0.21 average points over the strongest external baseline on Qwen3-8B and +0.16 on Llama3.1-8B. Component ablations show that confidence, coverage, and counterfactual signals all provide useful budget supervision, but their combination is not monotonically best on every backbone.</p></details> |  |
| **[MedRouter: Demystifying Knowledge Differences Across Medical LLMs for Routing-Based Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33119v1)** | 2026-09-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical question answering spans diverse specialties and modalities, and individual medical large language models (LLMs) exhibit distinct strengths across tasks and domains. This heterogeneity suggests that combining specialists may enable broader coverage of medical questions than relying on any single model. However, existing LLM routing methods primarily seek to balance answer quality and inference cost, leaving open how to exploit differences in specialist competence to improve medical reasoning. In this paper, we introduce MedRouter, an agentic system that uses an embedding-based multi-label router to select and query specialist LLMs, then passes their responses to a generator to produce the final answer. We further propose SCALE (Specialist Competence-Aware Learning), a two-stage training framework that first trains the Router with specialist correctness supervision and then optimizes its selections through reinforcement learning. The second stage uses a Performance Gain Reward (PGR) that measures how specialist information affects the generator's answer correctness relative to answering without that information. Experiments on eight text-based and multimodal medical QA benchmarks show that MedRouter outperforms the strongest routing baseline by 8% in average accuracy. Our analysis of specialist outputs further reveals distinct strengths and complementary question-level coverage, motivating learned routing to combine these capabilities for more comprehensive medical reasoning.</p></details> |  |

While medical LLMs excel in high-resource languages, their performance degrades sharply in Hindi, particularly on Indian systems of medicine. To this end, we introduce HiMed, a Hindi reasoning medical corpus and benchmark suite covering both Western and Indian medicine. We further propose HiMed-8B, a Hindi-form medical reasoning LLM, through the design of decaying scaffolding reward. Extensive experiments demonstrate improvement in Hindi medical reasoning performance and a reduction in the English-Hindi accuracy gap. All code, data, and weights are available</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[A General Multimodal Probing Framework for Data Fusion and Prediction with Frozen Large Language Models: Evidence from Multimodal EHR Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.28798v2)** | 2026-09-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The main objective of this paper is to propose a general framework for prediction based on different sources of multimodal data in the healthcare domain. The Combined terminal-layer probe reached 88.86% top-1 accuracy on MIMIC-IV, significantly outperforming CAML and PLM-ICD and statistically comparable to LAAT; Combined accuracy ranged from 87.69% to 89.25% across six backbones. | **[The widening evaluation gap in medical large language model research 2023 to 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11770v1)** | 2026-09-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models are superseded every few quarters; clinical evidence takes years. We asked whether medical research is keeping pace with the systems it evaluates. PubMed returned 11,628 records for January 2023 to June 2026 across fourteen clinical domains, growing 45-fold; 2.5% used a randomised, controlled or prospective design. Evaluation lag, from a study's newest named model release to its own publication, widened from 1.33 to 6.08 quarters. Because discontinued models age mechanically, we benchmarked this against a counterfactual holding model composition fixed: migration to newer systems offset only 56% of the drift (95% CI 50-65). Randomised trials evaluated models a median 4.6 quarters older than other designs (P = 3 x 10^-19), yet among studies naming a model still under development no design differed from any other; 62% of randomised trials evaluated a discontinued family. Rigour and currency are in tension, and that tension reflects model selection rather than research timelines.</p></details> |  |

Across seven open-weight LLMs, realistic noise reduces diagnostic accuracy by 15-25 percentage points and increases conversation length by 34-55%; the 7-8B models degrade 1.4x more than 70B+ models. A board-certified physician and a licensed nurse rate VeriSim's conversations highly on truth, realism, clinical utility, and noise fidelity (inter-annotator agreement >= 0.80 across all dimensions), and an LLM-as-judge closely tracks their ratings, enabling scalable evaluation. We release VeriSim as an open-source framework for rigorous stress-testing of clinical AI: https://github.com/mohitmarvania/VeriSim</p></details> | <details><summary>26 pa...</summary><p>26 pages, 9 figures, 13 tables. Accepted to Findings of EMNLP 2026</p></details> |

We demonstrate though this study the risk of SFT-stage poisoning, hoping to spur more studies of defense in the sensitive medical domain.</p></details> |  |
| **[DeVisE: Behavioral Testing of Medical Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15339v3)** | 2026-02-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied in clinical decision support, yet current evaluations rarely reveal whether their outputs reflect genuine medical reasoning or superficial correlations. We introduce DeVisE (Demographics and Vital signs Evaluation), a behavioral testing framework that probes fine-grained clinical understanding through controlled counterfactuals. Using intensive care unit (ICU) discharge notes from MIMIC-IV, we construct both raw (real-world) and template-based (synthetic) variants with single-variable perturbations in demographic (age, gender, ethnicity) and vital sign attributes. We evaluate eight LLMs, spanning general-purpose and medical variants, under zero-shot setting. Model behavior is analyzed through (1) input-level sensitivity, capturing how counterfactuals alter perplexity, and (2) downstream reasoning, measuring their effect on predicted ICU length-of-stay and mortality. Overall, our results show that standard task metrics obscure clinically relevant differences in model behavior, with models differing substantially in how consistently and proportionally they adjust predictions to counterfactual perturbations.</p></details> | <details><summary>Camer...</summary><p>Camera-ready version published at Findings of the EACL 2026</p></details> |
| **[PeruMedQA: Benchmarking Large Language Models (LLMs) on Peruvian Medical Exams -- Dataset Construction and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.11517v2)** | 2026-02-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>BACKGROUND: Medical large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance in answering medical examinations. However, the extent to which this high performance is transferable to medical questions in Spanish and from a Latin American country remains unexplored. This knowledge is crucial as LLM-based medical applications gain traction in Latin America. AIMS: To build a dataset of questions medical examinations taken by Peruvian physicians pursuing specialty training; to fine-tune a LLM on this dataset; to evaluate and compare the performance in terms of accuracy between vanilla LLMs and the fine-tuned LLM. METHODS: We curated PeruMedQA, a multiple-choice question-answering (MCQA) dataset containing 8,380 questions spanning 12 specialties (2018-2025). We selected ten medical LLMs, including medgemma-4b-it and medgemma-27b-text-it, and developed zero-shot task specific prompts to answer the questions. We employed parameter-efficient fine tuning (PEFT) and low-rand adaptation (LoRA) to fine-tune medgemma-4b-it utilizing all questions except those from 2025 (test set). RESULTS: Medgemma-27b showed the highest accuracy across all specialities, achieving the highest score of 89.29% in Psychiatry; yet, in two specialties, OctoMed-7B exhibited slight superiority: Neurosurgery with 77.27% and 77.38, respectively; and Radiology with 76.13% and 77.39%, respectively. Across specialties, most LLMs with <10 billion parameters exhibited <50% of correct answers. The fine-tuned version of medgemma-4b-it emerged victorious against all LLMs with <10 billion parameters and rivaled a LLM with 70 billion parameters across various examinations. CONCLUSIONS: For medical AI applications and research that require knowledge bases from Spanish-speaking countries and those exhibiting similar epidemiological profile to Peru's, interested parties should utilize medgemma-27b-text-it.</p></details> | <details><summary>https...</summary><p>https://github.com/rodrigo-carrillo/PeruMedQA</p></details> |
| **[MedDialogRubrics: A Comprehensive Benchmark and Evaluation Framework for Multi-turn Medical Consultations in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.03023v2)** | 2026-01-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical conversational AI (AI) plays a pivotal role in the development of safer and more effective medical dialogue systems. However, existing benchmarks and evaluation frameworks for assessing the information-gathering and diagnostic reasoning abilities of medical large language models (LLMs) have not been rigorously evaluated. To address these gaps, we present MedDialogRubrics, a novel benchmark comprising 5,200 synthetically constructed patient cases and over 60,000 fine-grained evaluation rubrics generated by LLMs and subsequently refined by clinical experts, specifically designed to assess the multi-turn diagnostic capabilities of LLM. Our framework employs a multi-agent system to synthesize realistic patient records and chief complaints from underlying disease knowledge without accessing real-world electronic health records, thereby mitigating privacy and data-governance concerns. We design a robust Patient Agent that is limited to a set of atomic medical facts and augmented with a dynamic guidance mechanism that continuously detects and corrects hallucinations throughout the dialogue, ensuring internal coherence and clinical plausibility of the simulated cases. Furthermore, we propose a structured LLM-based and expert-annotated rubric-generation pipeline that retrieves Evidence-Based Medicine (EBM) guidelines and utilizes the reject sampling to derive a prioritized set of rubric items ("must-ask" items) for each case. We perform a comprehensive evaluation of state-of-the-art models and demonstrate that, across multiple assessment dimensions, current models face substantial challenges. Our results indicate that improving medical dialogue will require advances in dialogue management architectures, not just incremental tuning of the base-model.</p></details> |  |
| **[Measuring Stability Beyond Accuracy in Small Open-Source Medical Large Language Models for Pediatric Endocrinology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.11567v1)** | 2025-12-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Small open-source medical large language models (LLMs) offer promising opportunities for low-resource deployment and broader accessibility. However, their evaluation is often limited to accuracy on medical multiple choice question (MCQ) benchmarks, and lacks evaluation of consistency, robustness, or reasoning behavior. We use MCQ coupled to human evaluation and clinical review to assess six small open-source medical LLMs (HuatuoGPT-o1 (Chen 2024), Diabetica-7B, Diabetica-o1 (Wei 2024), Meditron3-8B (Sallinen2025), MedFound-7B (Liu 2025), and ClinicaGPT-base-zh (Wang 2023)) in pediatric endocrinology. In deterministic settings, we examine the effect of prompt variation on models' output and self-assessment bias. In stochastic settings, we evaluate output variability and investigate the relationship between consistency and correctness. HuatuoGPT-o1-8B achieved the highest performance. The results show that high consistency across the model response is not an indicator of correctness, although HuatuoGPT-o1-8B showed the highest consistency rate. When tasked with selecting correct reasoning, both HuatuoGPT-o1-8B and Diabetica-o1 exhibit self-assessment bias and dependency on the order of the candidate explanations. Expert review of incorrect reasoning rationales identified a mix of clinically acceptable responses and clinical oversight. We further show that system-level perturbations, such as differences in CUDA builds, can yield statistically significant shifts in model output despite stable accuracy.
